========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yeah—thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that I can’t seem to organize all the moving parts in my life anymore. It’s like there are too many open loops at once—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions—and I keep thinking I’ll sit down and make a clear plan, but then I end up just grabbing whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment.

And the frustrating part is that the loudest thing isn’t always the most important thing. So I’ll spend time on something because it’s right in front of me, or someone is asking about it, while the thing that actually has a deadline or needs careful thought sort of sits there. I’m not trying to be careless—I can see all the pieces, I just can’t seem to arrange them in a way that holds together long enough to work through them properly. That’s really why I’m here.

Yeah, looking back, I think it was there pretty early. At the time it didn’t feel like “a thing,” exactly—I was just the kid who was supposedly smart but kind of off in his own world. I remember teachers writing comments like that, that I was bright, or understood the material, but I daydreamed too much.

And the part that really stands out is I’d sometimes actually do the homework and then not turn it in. Like it would be in the wrong folder, or left at home, or I’d lose the folder entirely. My parents would get frustrated because it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done it—it was that I couldn’t reliably get it from “finished” to “handed in.” That was definitely happening in grade school, well before I was a teenager.

Yeah, definitely. It’s not just a work thing. Work is where it becomes the most visible, I guess, because there are deadlines and other people waiting on me, but the same pattern shows up everywhere.

Like, I’ll have a bunch of things floating around at once—some practical, some creative, some family or personal—and I keep thinking, okay, I need to sit down and make a real plan. But then I don’t quite get it into a shape I can follow. I’ll start with whatever is most in my face or whatever feels urgent in that minute, even if it’s not actually the thing that matters most. And then the bigger thing, the thing that needed steady attention, just sort of sits there.

That’s honestly what pushed me to come in. It feels like I can see all the moving parts—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions, all these separate threads—but I can’t reliably organize them and move through them in order. I end up reacting instead of directing things, if that makes sense.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes—very easily. That’s actually one of the most frustrating parts because it doesn’t always feel like “I got bored and chose to stop.” It’s more like the thread just gets pulled out of my hands.

If I’m coding and I’m holding some structure in my head—like what a function is supposed to do, or how two pieces are supposed to connect—and then Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some visual idea pops into my head, I can completely lose the line I was following. And then instead of just noticing it and coming back, I’ll somehow be twenty minutes into some unrelated design rabbit hole, looking at interface examples or sketching out a concept that wasn’t the thing I sat down to work on.

The annoying part is that the distraction can be tiny. It doesn’t have to be some huge interruption. Sometimes it’s just one little notification or one thought like, “Oh, that layout could be interesting,” and suddenly the original coding problem feels far away, like I have to reconstruct where I was from scratch.

It doesn’t have to be some huge interruption. Sometimes it’s just one little notification or one thought like, “Oh, that layout could be interesting,” and suddenly the original coding problem feels far away, like I have to reconstruct where I was from scratch.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you spend a lot of time worrying, even when there isn’t one specific problem right in front of you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / software_developer / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I worry like anyone does, I think, especially when there are a lot of things going on, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as this constant worrying when there isn’t a specific problem.

Most of the time, if I’m anxious or tense, it feels tied to something concrete—like something I haven’t dealt with, or something I’m behind on, or some loose end I know exists even if I’m avoiding looking directly at it. I don’t usually sit there thinking, “Something bad is going to happen,” out of nowhere. It’s more that my mind keeps circling unfinished things, if that makes sense.

But if you mean a general background hum of worry with no clear object, I don’t think that’s a huge thing for me. I’d say it’s more situational.

I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve definitely had stretches where I felt discouraged or kind of worn down, especially when things were piling up or I felt like I wasn’t keeping up, but “down or depressed more days than not for years” doesn’t feel accurate to me.

It’s more like my mood dips in response to things going badly or feeling unmanageable, and then if something goes better, or I get a little momentum, I can feel more like myself again. I wouldn’t say there’s been this steady, long-term depressed feeling sitting there most days for a couple of years.

No, I don’t think so. Looking back, it really doesn’t feel like it started after some big stressful life change. I mean, stress can definitely make it more obvious, but the pattern was there way before adulthood.

My parents would get frustrated because it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done it—it was that I couldn’t reliably get it from “finished” to “handed in.” That was definitely happening in grade school, well before I was a teenager.\n\nDoctor: Do these same problems with organization and follow-through also affect you outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just a work thing. Work is where it becomes the most visible, I guess, because there are deadlines and other people waiting on me, but the same pattern shows up everywhere.\n\nLike, I’ll have a bunch of things floating around at once—some practical, some creative, some family or personal—and I keep thinking, okay, I need to sit down and make a real plan. But then I don’t quite get it into a shape I can follow. I’ll start with whatever is most in my face or whatever feels urgent in that minute, even if it’s not actually the thing that matters most. And then the bigger thing, the thing that needed steady attention, just sort of sits there.\n\nThat’s honestly what pushed me to come in. It feels like I can see all the moving parts—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions, all these separate threads—but I can’t reliably organize them and move through them in order. I end up reacting instead of directing things, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you get distracted easily while you're trying to focus?\n\nPatient: Yes—very easily. That’s actually one of the most frustrating parts because it doesn’t always feel like “I got bored and chose to stop.” It’s more like the thread just gets pulled out of my hands.\n\nIf I’m coding and I’m holding some structure in my head—like what a function is supposed to do, or how two pieces are supposed to connect—and then Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some visual idea pops into my head, I can completely lose the line I was following. And then instead of just noticing it and coming back, I’ll somehow be twenty minutes into some unrelated design rabbit hole, looking at interface examples or sketching out a concept that wasn’t the thing I sat down to work on.\n\nThe annoying part is that the distraction can be tiny. It doesn’t have to be some huge interruption. Sometimes it’s just one little notification or one thought like, “Oh, that layout could be interesting,” and suddenly the original coding problem feels far away, like I have to reconstruct where I was from scratch.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying, even when there isn’t one specific problem right in front of you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I worry like anyone does, I think, especially when there are a lot of things going on, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as this constant worrying when there isn’t a specific problem.\n\nMost of the time, if I’m anxious or tense, it feels tied to something concrete—like something I haven’t dealt with, or something I’m behind on, or some loose end I know exists even if I’m avoiding looking directly at it. I don’t usually sit there thinking, “Something bad is going to happen,” out of nowhere. It’s more that my mind keeps circling unfinished things, if that makes sense.\n\nBut if you mean a general background hum of worry with no clear object, I don’t think that’s a huge thing for me. I’d say it’s more situational.\n\nDoctor: For at least a couple of years, have you felt down or depressed more days than not?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve definitely had stretches where I felt discouraged or kind of worn down, especially when things were piling up or I felt like I wasn’t keeping up, but “down or depressed more days than not for years” doesn’t feel accurate to me.\n\nIt’s more like my mood dips in response to things going badly or feeling unmanageable, and then if something goes better, or I get a little momentum, I can feel more like myself again. I wouldn’t say there’s been this steady, long-term depressed feeling sitting there most days for a couple of years.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems start after a major stressful change in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Looking back, it really doesn’t feel like it started after some big stressful life change. I mean, stress can definitely make it more obvious, but the pattern was there way before adulthood.\n\nI’ve actually seen comments from my grade school teachers that were kind of embarrassingly consistent—things like I was bright, but I daydreamed a lot, or I didn’t turn in assignments even when I’d done them. I remember losing homework folders and papers when I was still pretty young, long before I was a teenager. So it’s not like everything was fine and then some major event happened and suddenly these problems appeared. It feels more like it’s always been part of how I operated, just with different consequences at different ages.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Symptoms did not begin after a stressor, making F43.2 unlikely; chronic generalized worry and persistent depressive mood were also not supported.","is_final":true}

Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYes—very easily. That’s actually one of the most frustrating parts because it doesn’t always feel like “I got bored and chose to stop.” It’s more like the thread just gets pulled out of my hands.\n\nIf I’m coding and I’m holding some structure in my head—like what a function is supposed to do, or how two pieces are supposed to connect—and then Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some visual idea pops into my head, I can completely lose the line I was following. And then instead of just noticing it and coming back, I’ll somehow be twenty minutes into some unrelated design rabbit hole, looking at interface examples or sketching out a concept that wasn’t the thing I sat down to work on.\n\nThe annoying part is that the distraction can be tiny. It doesn’t have to be some huge interruption. Sometimes it’s just one little notification or one thought like, “Oh, that layout could be interesting,” and suddenly the original coding problem feels far away, like I have to reconstruct where I was from scratch.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. It’s more that my mind keeps circling unfinished things, if that makes sense.\n\nBut if you mean a general background hum of worry with no clear object, I don’t think that’s a huge thing for me. Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.
